# Architecture Decision Record

This file records decisions that should not be silently changed.

## ADR-001 — Director OS baseline

**Decision:** Minimum supported version is **3.3.0**.

**Why:** OS 3.3 provides a cleaner modern DriverWorks baseline while keeping compatibility with the older OS 3 family that DirectorLink targets.

## ADR-002 — Installation is out of scope

**Decision:** DirectorLink assumes `DirectorLink.c4z` is installed. The project does not care how the user obtained permission/access to install it.

**Consequence:** No jailbreak dependency and no dealer-specific runtime dependency.

## ADR-003 — Director is the runtime dependency

**Decision:** DirectorLink talks to Director from inside a DriverWorks driver.

**Rejected:** Building the core around Composer Pro or the external `/api/v1` Director REST interface.

## ADR-004 — Cloudflare frontend, local control

**Decision:** Host the PWA on Cloudflare Pages, but have the browser connect directly to DirectorLink on the LAN.

**Consequence:** Browser Local Network Access permission and correct CORS/private-network handling are part of onboarding.

**Superseded in part by ADR-026 (0.8.0):** the sites are Cloudflare Workers static assets, deployed by `deploy.yml`.

## ADR-005 — LAN only in V1

**Decision:** No DirectorLink-hosted remote-control relay in V1.

**Superseded by ADR-029 (0.10.0):** optional remote access through api.directorlink.io, sealed end to end.

## ADR-006 — One owner

**Decision:** One paired owner identity/account in V1. Multi-user/roles are deferred.

**Superseded by ADR-025, ADR-027 and ADR-029:** keys have roles, the owner pairs and everyone else is invited.

## ADR-007 — Adapter-based devices

**Decision:** Normalize devices and add explicit proxy-family adapters. Unknown devices remain unsupported rather than receiving guessed commands.

## ADR-008 — DirectorLink owns automation

**Decision:** Scenes, schedules, and automations are DirectorLink-native. Do not import Composer programming/schedules/scenes.

## ADR-009 — Internal scheduler

**Decision:** Do not depend on the Composer Scheduler Agent as the primary automation engine. DirectorLink will persist schedules and execute actions itself using Director timers/time/location APIs.

## ADR-010 — No automatic C4Z update in V1

**Decision:** Updates are manual through Composer initially.

**Extended by ADR-035 (1.1.0):** admins see in the app when a newer release is out, with its download and the Composer steps; installing stays in Composer.

## ADR-011 — Discovery source

**Decision:** Prefer structured DriverWorks tables:
- `C4:GetDevices({})`
- `C4:GetProjectHierarchy()`

rather than parsing the entire project XML when structured APIs already provide the required data.

**Reason:** Less fragile and easier to normalize across versions.

## ADR-012 — User-facing entity identity

**Decision:** Proxy entities are the primary homeowner-facing devices. Backing protocol drivers are retained as relationship metadata and not shown as duplicate controllable entities.

Standalone/combo drivers without proxy relationships may appear as unsupported entities.

## ADR-013 — Apache-2.0

**Decision:** DirectorLink uses Apache License 2.0.


## ADR-014 — GitHub Releases are the binary distribution channel

**Decision:** Do not commit built `DirectorLink.c4z` binaries to `main`. Publish official binaries as versioned GitHub Release assets.

**Why:** Users can clearly upgrade/downgrade, release binaries stay tied to immutable tags, and source history remains clean.

**Release contents:** `DirectorLink.c4z`, `SHA256SUMS.txt`, and release notes.

**Version source:** root `VERSION` file using semantic versioning. Versions with a prerelease suffix such as `-alpha.1` are published as prereleases.

**Since changed (ADR-022):** releases also carry `openapi.json`, and `VERSION` is plain `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH`, without prerelease suffixes.

**Since changed (ADR-035):** releases up to 1.0.0 were published mutable: their files could still be replaced after publishing. From 1.1.0 on they are immutable, with the repository's immutable-releases setting on: once a release is published, its tag and files cannot change. The app's update notice offers only immutable releases.


## ADR-015 — Start the PWA without a frontend framework

**Decision:** The first DirectorLink PWA is plain HTML, CSS, and JavaScript hosted directly from the repository `app/` directory.

**Why:** The current UI is small, this removes Node/framework dependencies from deployment, and it lets us prove the harder browser-to-LAN transport before committing to a larger frontend stack.

**Cloudflare configuration:** root `web`, build command `exit 0`, output directory `.`. (Superseded by ADR-026: `app/`, `console/` and `site/` are deployed by `deploy.yml`.)

**Revisit:** A framework/build tool may be introduced later if the device dashboard, state management, routing, or component complexity justifies it.


## ADR-016 — Read-only HTTP transport spike on port 41999

**Decision:** Validate browser-to-Director communication with a minimal HTTP/1.1 server implemented on DriverWorks `C4:CreateServer`, listening on fixed TCP port `41999`.

**Why fixed port:** A public browser cannot discover Director's random ephemeral DriverWorks socket port. A known port gives the PWA a deterministic local endpoint without LAN scanning.

**Security:** Read-only routes require a random per-install Bearer token persisted encrypted on Director. CORS is restricted to official DirectorLink origins.

**Status:** Alpha integration decision. Re-evaluate port configurability and final pairing/authentication after testing on real systems.

**Superseded by ADR-019 in 0.2.0.** The fixed port 41999 stays; the read-only alpha routes were replaced by the OpenAPI contract.


## ADR-017 — Control lights through the Light V2 proxy

**Decision:** DirectorLink controls lighting entities by their Light V2 **proxy IDs**, not by sending commands directly to backing protocol drivers.

**State contract:**
- variable 1000 = Light State
- variable 1001 = Light Brightness Percent when the device is dimmable
- variable 1006 = Default On Preset Brightness when available

**Control contract:** On/Off use `SET_BRIGHTNESS_TARGET` with static preset IDs 1/2 and are validated on a real Director. Explicit brightness is adapter-selected: `knx_dimmer.c4i` uses DriverWorks-documented `RAMP_TO_LEVEL` (`LEVEL`, `TIME = 0`); other Light V2 dimmers currently use `SET_BRIGHTNESS_TARGET` with `PERCENT`.

**Why:** The proxy is Control4's abstraction boundary. It lets DirectorLink support Control4, Zigbee, Z-Wave and third-party lighting drivers through one documented interface instead of learning each protocol driver's private command set.

**Safety:** A device is not marked controllable unless its Light V2 state variable exists and DirectorLink can register the required state listener.

**Extended by ADR-033 (1.1.0):** the legacy Light proxy (`light.c4i`) is controlled the same way, through its proxy, with its own commands.

**Changed by ADR-077 (1.10.2):** a level goes to every Light V2 dimmer as `SET_BRIGHTNESS_TARGET` with `LIGHT_BRIGHTNESS_TARGET` and `RATE = 0` (no more `RAMP_TO_LEVEL` or `PERCENT`), and a light is a dimmer when its driver declares `<dimmer>` or `<set_level>` True; variable 1001 decides only when its driver declares neither.


## ADR-018 — One-owner local pairing

**Decision:** V1 uses a short local pairing code to provision one long-lived owner Bearer credential to a browser.

**Owner credential:**
- generated randomly with `C4:UUID("RANDOM")`
- persisted encrypted on Director
- never displayed in Composer after alpha.8
- stored locally by each paired browser

**Pairing code:**
- 8 numeric digits
- visible in the DirectorLink Composer properties
- valid for 15 minutes
- rotated after every successful pairing
- five failed attempts per minute trigger a 60-second lock
- never persisted by the PWA

**Transport:** `POST /v1/pair` is the only unauthenticated application route. The code is sent in the `X-DirectorLink-Pairing-Code` header, not in a URL.

**Why:** Homeowners should not copy long API secrets from Composer. Pairing keeps the long credential private while preserving the local-first, no-cloud-relay architecture.

**Superseded by ADR-021 in 0.2.0.** Pairing now issues named API keys instead of one shared owner credential, and the code moved from a header to the JSON body of `POST /v1/auth/pair`.


## ADR-019 — OpenAPI-first REST API with logical names

**Decision:** `api/openapi.yaml` (OpenAPI 3.1) is the contract for the LAN API. Resources use logical names — `rooms`, `devices`, `lights`, `thermostats`, `logs`, `api-keys` — and state changes are `PATCH` requests with the desired state (`{"on": true}`), answered with `202 Accepted`. Errors use RFC 9457 Problem Details with a stable `code`.

**Why:** A standard description lets any client (the app, Postman, Home Assistant, scripts) use the API without knowing Control4. Control4 command names, proxy IDs and variable numbers stay inside the adapters.

**Consequences:**
- `scripts/check_api.py` fails CI when the spec and `driver/src/api/routes.lua` differ in any method, path or public flag.
- The build embeds the spec so the driver serves it at `/v1/openapi.json`; releases publish it as `openapi.json`.
- The driver uses its own JSON encoder/decoder (`src/core/json.lua`) for deterministic output (explicit `[]` and `null`) and so the API layer can be tested in plain Lua.
- The HTTP server runs without a delimiter and assembles requests itself, so request bodies (`Content-Length`) are supported.

## ADR-020 — One repository for API, driver and app

**Decision:** The API contract, driver and app stay in this repository (`api/`, `driver/`, `app/`), with the app deployed by Cloudflare from `app/`.

**Why:** While the API is changing, most changes touch the spec, driver and app together; one pull request keeps them consistent and CI checks them together. The Jewish-calendar module (later) and the automation engine ship inside the `.c4z` anyway.

**Revisit:** Split a part into its own repository when it gets an independent lifecycle or other consumers (most likely a reusable calendar library, then the app).

## ADR-021 — API keys

**Decision:** Every route except health, the API description and pairing requires `Authorization: Bearer <api key>`. Keys are named, stored on Director (at most 20; encrypted up to 0.8.0, only hashes since 0.9.2 — ADR-028), listed without secrets, and revocable through the API or all at once with the Composer action **Revoke All API Keys**.

**First key (0.2.0):** exchange the 8-digit Composer pairing code at `POST /v1/auth/pair` (15-minute code, rotated after use, 5 failures per minute lock pairing for 60 seconds).

**0.3.0–0.7.0 (replaced by ADR-027):** the first key came from approval in the Control4 app: the driver adds a **DirectorLink Access** experience button (a `uibutton` proxy on binding 5001); a client calls `POST /v1/auth/requests`, the homeowner presses the button within 2 minutes, and the client collects its key once with the secret request id. One request waits at a time, 5 per 10 minutes. The Composer pairing code stays as a fallback.

**Why keys and not an open LAN API:** the API can operate door, gate and garage relays through KNX; without a key anything on the home network could.

## ADR-024 — DirectorLink is a protocol driver with a button proxy, not a combo driver

**Superseded by ADR-027 (0.8.0):** with the button gone, DirectorLink is a combo driver again.

**Decision (0.3.0):** `driver.xml` declares no `<combo>`; the DirectorLink protocol device runs the Lua code and owns one `uibutton` proxy on binding 5001 (**DirectorLink Access**).

**Why:** Real-system testing showed that for a combo driver the Director creates only the combined device and never the extra button proxy. Every working button driver on the test system (door and garage relays, DoorBird, experience buttons) is a protocol device (item type 6) with a `uibutton` child proxy (type 7).

**Consequence:** Moving from the 0.2.x combo layout needs a one-time remove and re-add in Composer. `check_package.py` fails if `<combo>` comes back. Discovery skips the bridge's own proxies.

## ADR-022 — Versions are MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH with one source

**Decision:** `VERSION` holds a plain `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` version with no pre-release suffix and is the only version to edit. The build stamps it into `version.lua`, the API description and `driver.xml`, where Control4's integer version is derived as `MAJOR*10000 + MINOR*100 + PATCH`.

**Why:** One place to change, and the Control4 driver version always increases with the release version. `0.x` releases already signal that the project is in development.

## ADR-023 — Driver log served by the API

**Decision:** The driver keeps its last 500 log entries in memory and serves them at `GET /v1/logs` (filters by level, category and sequence number); the level is set with `PATCH /v1/logs/settings` or the Composer **Log Level** property. Entries also go to the Director driver log.

**Scope:** DirectorLink's own log only. Director's system logs are outside the driver sandbox and contain other drivers' data, so they are not exposed over the LAN. Fields such as keys, tokens and pairing codes are redacted before logging.

## ADR-025 — API keys have roles; doors need a Composer switch

**Superseded by ADR-054 (1.8.0):** two roles per person, admin and member, instead of four per key; keys keep a 1.7.0 role worked out from their person.

**Context:** One kind of key could do everything, including opening doors and creating more keys. Remote access (next) and family members need less than that.

**Decision (0.7.0):** Four ordered roles — `viewer`, `member`, `doors`, `admin` — each allowed everything the ones before it are. Every non-public route in `routes.lua` names the least role it needs, and the OpenAPI operation states the same as `x-directorlink-role`; `check_api.py` fails the build when they differ or when a restricted operation does not document 403. Keys from before roles existed become `admin`; the Composer pairing code issues `admin`; access requests default to `admin` for a home's first key and `member` afterwards, and Composer shows the requested role before the homeowner presses the button. The last admin key cannot be demoted. Opening doors additionally needs the Composer property **Door Control** = Enabled (default Disabled).

**Consequence:** Remote users will map onto the same roles. The app should read `GET /v1/api-keys/current` and hide what the key may not do.

## ADR-026 — The project is DirectorLink, on directorlink.io

**Context:** "C4Bridge" leaned on the Control4 name and the project needed an international brand before it has outside users and accounts. The API console had grown into a developer tool of its own inside the app.

**Decision (0.8.0):** The project, driver and API are named **DirectorLink** (slogan: *Direct to Director. End-to-end integration. Open source.*), on `directorlink.io`. The driver package is `DirectorLink.c4z` and its button **DirectorLink Access**. The repository holds three static sites, each its own Cloudflare Worker with a custom domain: `app/` (formerly `web/`) on `app.directorlink.io`, `console/` (API console, debugging, logs, key management) on `console.directorlink.io`, and `site/` (landing page) on `directorlink.io`. GitHub Actions deploys them (`deploy.yml`) instead of dashboard-configured builds, so the deployment is part of the repository. Past release notes and the validation log keep the old name as written.

**Consequence:** Control4 sees DirectorLink as a different driver: moving from C4Bridge means removing it and adding DirectorLink, so API keys, room names and the Door Control setting start fresh. The driver's CORS allowlist is the two new sites plus localhost (localhost removed in 1.0.0, ADR-032); the old `app.c4bridge.io` no longer reaches a DirectorLink controller.

## ADR-027 — Owners pair once with an on-demand code; everyone else is invited

**Context:** 0.3.0–0.7.0 approved each new browser with a button in the Control4 app. That needed a child proxy (so DirectorLink could not be a single device), a workaround to make the button visible, and an approval for every phone. With accounts and remote access coming, only the owner should ever need to prove control of the project.

**Decision (0.8.0):** The button and access requests are removed; DirectorLink is again a single self-contained device (combo driver with its own proxy). The first key comes from a pairing code created on demand with the Composer action **New Pairing Code** (automatically while the driver has no keys): shown as `1234 5678`, valid 15 minutes, works once, rate-limited (5 wrong codes → 60 s lock), and always `admin` — Composer access already means full control. Further keys are created by an admin. With remote access (planned): the owner signs in with Google or Apple and claims the home on the LAN with a pairing code; family members join by an invitation tied to the invited email (single use, 7 days; the owner approves when the accepting account's email differs); roles are the same as for API keys. Home-network use without an account stays possible.

**Consequence:** Installers read the code for homeowners without Composer. Replaces the approval flow of ADR-021 and the protocol-driver-with-button layout of ADR-024. The Composer properties are reduced to what an installer needs; the load history moves to `GET /v1/system` and the console.

## ADR-028 — Stored data is written as `json:` plus JSON; keys are kept as hashes

**Context:** Up to 0.9.1, DirectorLink lost its API keys and its remote-access identity whenever the driver reloaded, and room names were read by the same kind of code. On the test controller (OS 3.4.3) the values were saved — they are in Director's `state.db` — but `C4:PersistGetValue` returned a stored JSON string decoded into a Lua table, and DirectorLink accepted only a string, so it started empty. Values that are not JSON (the load and pairing counters) came back as written, which hid the problem. The keys that 0.8.0 kept encrypted were lost on update too; whether they also came back decoded or could not be decrypted (Director logs "has no driverKey" at every load of the driver) was not established.

**Decision (0.9.2):** `src/core/store.lua` writes every stored value as `json:` followed by JSON, which Director returns unchanged, and accepts tables Director has already decoded, so data written by 0.9.1 and older is read and then rewritten. Keys are kept in plain persistence as SHA-256 hashes (SHA-1 on a controller without SHA-256; each key records its algorithm). A presented key is hashed and compared in constant time; the key itself exists only in the response that creates it. Keys in the encrypted store of 0.8.0 and 0.9.0 are moved when Director can read that store, which is then emptied. The remote-access identity is kept in plain persistence as well: its secret has to be sent to the relay, so it cannot be kept as a hash.

**Consequence:** Keys, room names and the home identity survive driver updates and restarts, and each load logs how the keys came back (`keys loaded`, `stored_as`). Whoever can read the driver's stored data (root on the controller, possibly a project backup) finds only hashes of long random keys, which cannot be turned back into keys. The home secret is readable there; that is acceptable while remote access is a read-only test, and is revisited when claiming a home with a pairing code replaces trust on first use. The fake Director in the driver tests decodes stored JSON the same way. (Since ADR-029 each key's lock key is stored too; ADR-032 lets the owner replace the home secret and says what such a copy allows.)

## ADR-081 — Workers Logs keeps DirectorLink's own lines, not one for every request

**Context:** The Worker's log (Workers Logs, `cloud/wrangler.jsonc` observability) is billed by the line beyond 20 million a month. On 2026-10-10, 100 lines read from it covered 10 minutes: 81 were Cloudflare's invocation log (one for every request and every WebSocket message the controller sent), 18 were `message_relayed` (one for every relayed message), and 1 was a line worth reading (`driver_disconnected`). At 1,000 homes and 3,000 users that is about 70 million lines a month, about $31 of a $50–55 bill.

**Decision (1.11.0):**
- **No invocation logs:** `observability.logs.invocation_logs` false. DirectorLink's own lines (each event one JSON line, `cloud/README.md`) stay, all of them.
- **`message_relayed` only when it says something:** a relayed message the home refused (`ok` false, with its `code`) or that took 3 s or more (`SLOW_MESSAGE_MS`). A quick, answered message leaves no line; its failures (`message_timeout`, `message_failed`) are logged as before.
- **Errors keep a line:** an error thrown while the relay handles the driver's socket message is logged (`socket_message_failed`, with its stack) and thrown as before; the Worker's own catch (`worker_error`) and the relay's alarm (`alert_alarm_failed`) already logged theirs. The dashboard's metrics still count requests and errors without logs.

**Why:** The lines that found the relay's drops, checked alert deliveries and the iPhone's registration are DirectorLink's own and rare; the others only said that something happened.

**Consequence:** About 1 % of the lines, inside the included 20 million at 1,000 homes. No longer in the log: each request's method, URL and status, and how long each quick message took. A Worker change only: no driver, app or D1 change.

## ADR-077 — A light dims when its driver says so; a level goes as Light V2 documents it (#11)

**Context:** Two problems with the owner's KNX lights, read on his controller (OS 4.2.1) on 2026-10-09. (1) 1.10.1 took every Light V2 light with a level variable (1001) for a dimmer, but the Light V2 proxy gives switches that variable too: the owner's 107 KNX switches (`knx_switch.c4i`) report 0 or 100 there, so each showed a 0–100 % slider that could only turn it on or off. Their driver says what they are in its driver.xml: `<dimmer>False</dimmer>` and `<set_level>False</set_level>`; the KNX dimmer's driver (`knx_dimmer.c4i`) says True for both, with `<supports_target>True</supports_target>`. (2) KNX dimmers did not dim from DirectorLink (#11, open since alpha.7). DirectorLink sent `SET_BRIGHTNESS_TARGET` with `PERCENT` (since alpha.6) and, to `knx_dimmer.c4i`, `RAMP_TO_LEVEL` with `LEVEL` and `TIME = 0` (since alpha.7). The KNX dimmer's driver takes the level of `SET_BRIGHTNESS_TARGET` from `LIGHT_BRIGHTNESS_TARGET` (0–100), with an optional ramp `RATE` (ms) and preset id (its On and Off presets: 100 and 0); it never reads `PERCENT`. Snap One's Light V2 documentation names the same two for drivers with `supports_target` (the proxy gives older drivers `LEVEL` and `TIME`). Alpha.5 did send `LIGHT_BRIGHTNESS_TARGET` with `RATE = 0` and was replaced by alpha.6 the same afternoon, but the snapshot that judged it also showed the controller loading another copy of the driver file (`C4Bridge (1).c4z`, docs/RESEARCH.md): it is not known that alpha.5 ever ran there.

**Decision (1.10.2):**
- **Dimmer or switch by what the driver declares** (`src/control4/light_capabilities.lua`, for both `light_v2.lua` and the legacy `light_v1.lua`). A light dims when its protocol driver declares `<dimmer>` or `<set_level>` True, and is a switch when it declares them and neither is True. When Director gives neither (nothing declared, an error, no protocol driver), the rule of 1.10.1 stays: a level variable makes a dimmer. Read with `C4:GetDeviceData(<protocol driver id>, "capabilities")` (documented from OS 2.10; the `light_v2` proxy is Control4's own, the same for every light), else the whole `<devicedata>` (its `<capabilities>` only) when that gives no text; escaped text, comments, an attribute and True/true/1/yes are read, an error, nothing, a table or a number is nothing. Once per driver file name (lower case) and project read: the 107 lights on `knx_switch.c4i` cost one Director call. Logged once a file at Info (what it declares, and the shape of Director's answer, never its text). A driver updated in Composer is read again when its devices are set up again (`forget`, called by `Manager.setUpAgain` before it sets them up).
- **A switch** is on and off only: no `set_brightness` action; in the API `dimmable` false and `brightness` null (a PATCH with a brightness: 409 `NOT_SUPPORTED`, nothing sent); its level variable is not watched (Light State says the same); a scene's level turns it on. The app already did the rest for `dimmable` false: no slider in its room, "On" on Home's favorites, no Dim in the scene editor unless a dimmer is picked too (an action saved with a level opens as On), and "dim the kitchen pendant to 50%" answers "Kitchen pendant only turns on and off."
- **A level** goes to every Light V2 dimmer, the KNX dimmer too, as `SET_BRIGHTNESS_TARGET` with `LIGHT_BRIGHTNESS_TARGET` = the level and `RATE = 0` (at once), logged as before. `RAMP_TO_LEVEL` and `PERCENT` are gone. On and off stay the presets 1 and 2 (`LIGHT_BRIGHTNESS_TARGET_PRESET_ID`), which work.
- **Not `PERCENT` as well.** No driver written to Snap One's documentation reads it, and nothing was ever seen to move by it; if the proxy itself read `PERCENT` (alpha.6 saw the Control4 app's command carry it), sending both could let it win over the documented parameter, the very thing this fixes.
- **The KNX dimmer's level.** In alpha.6 its level variable did not follow the Control4 app's changes, only a full On, so it starts as not reported (the app shows the level it sent, marked as not reported, and does not wait for a confirmation it might not get) until the variable gives a level between 0 and 100 (at start or later), which only a dimmer that reports its level does: from then on it counts as reported, until the next project read.

**Why:** What a driver declares is what the Control4 app goes by, and it costs one Director call a driver file. The documented parameters are the ones the KNX dimmer's driver reads; the others were guesses (ADR-017's "compatibility path").

**Consequence:** The owner's 107 KNX switches (the heaters among them) lose their slider; scenes that set a level on a switch turn it on, as they did for switches before. Light V2 dimmers in every home get `LIGHT_BRIGHTNESS_TARGET` instead of `PERCENT`. A driver that turns itself into a dimmer only while it runs (Snap One's dynamic capabilities, `DYNAMIC_CAPABILITIES_CHANGED`) is what its driver.xml says: one whose driver.xml says switch shows no slider. Nothing stored changes: a 1.10.1 app shows what the driver says (it already hid the slider for `dimmable` false), a 1.10.2 app on a 1.10.1 driver shows what 1.10.1 said, and going back to 1.10.1 brings back the sliders and the old commands. What the cloud and the controller learn: nothing new (one Director read of each light driver's capabilities, on the controller). Not seen yet: a KNX dimmer moving by `LIGHT_BRIGHTNESS_TARGET` on the owner's controller, and whether its level then follows (the owner's test of 1.10.2).

**Tests and tools.** `driver/tests/test_light_v2.lua` (switch or dimmer by the declaration and without one, Director's odd answers, one read per driver file and project read with 107 switches, a driver update, the exact command, the KNX dimmer's level), `test_light_v1.lua`, `test_api.lua`, `test_scenes.lua`; `tests/app/light-switches.test.mjs`. The fake project (`Mock.project`) gives the KNX switch a level of 0 and both KNX drivers their capabilities, so `scripts/dev_server.py` shows a switch and dimmers as the owner's.

**Changed (2026-10-09, after 1.10.2, the owner's decision): a level for a room or the whole home goes to its dimmers only.** A simulation of the owner's home showed what "a scene's level turns it on" does there: a scene step that sets a percentage on a room or the whole home turns on every switch in it, among them a KNX switch that is really the door lock ("מנעול דלתות") and the heaters (boilers, floor heating: ADR-066, `app/js/heaters.js`). On 1.10.1 such a step did nothing to them (the KNX switch's driver never read `PERCENT`).
- **Room and home steps.** A lights step with a `brightness` above 0 and no `device_ids` sets the dimmers of its room (or of the home) and leaves each light there that is not one (`dimmable` false) as it is: skipped with `ON_OFF_ONLY` ("This light only turns on and off; a level for a room or the whole home goes to dimmers only"), never failed. A switch a step names by its id still turns on, as in 1.10.2; `on: true`, `on: false` and a level of 0 still go to every light, switches too; Home's Turn off all is unchanged. It is one rule where a step's commands are made (`deviceCommands`, `src/api/handlers/scenes.lua`), so it holds wherever a scene runs: from the app, Try it now, schedules, scene links, and commands that run a scene. A command that sets a room's level itself already leaves switches out (the app, since 1.10.2).
- **Saying it.** A run counts them in `skipped` and in `on_off_only`, every one, while `problems` lists at most 50: theirs come after the others, so the owner's 107 switches never crowd a failure out. History takes `counts.on_off_only` from the run (a user's, a schedule's or a link's); a link's run that left only switches is `ran`, not `partly`; a schedule's run alerts nobody for them. Composer: Last Automation "1 device, 1 switch left as it was", the printout "all lights in Living Room (11) -> 50%, dimmers only". `features.scene_levels_dimmers_only` in `GET /v1/system` says the driver does it.
- **The app**, with such a driver: Add an action says "Only dimmers get a percentage; switches stay as they are." while a level for the room or the whole home has switches there (not for lights picked one by one, where a switch turns on); the action and the scene's card say "All dimmers" and "Kitchen dimmers"; a run that left only switches is done, not partly done ("Done — only dimmers get a percentage; 3 switches stayed as they were"); History says "3 switches left as they were", apart from what was skipped. In English, Hebrew, Spanish and Italian.
- **Other versions.** Nothing stored changes, so nothing is migrated. A 1.10.2 app with this driver shows such a run as "Done — 3 devices were skipped" (History "3 skipped"); this app with a 1.10.2 driver shows nothing new, and that driver turns the switches on. Going back to 1.10.2 turns them on again. What the cloud and the controller learn: nothing new (the run's new count travels sealed, as every answer).

**Tests** of the change: `driver/tests/test_scenes.lua` (a room's and the whole home's level, legacy lights and a member's run too, a switch named, off, 0 and on, 61 switches past the 50), `test_schedules.lua` (a schedule, no alert, Composer), `test_scene_links.lua`; `tests/app/light-switches.test.mjs`; `scripts/check_contract.py`.

## ADR-076 — Each thermostat in its own scale, as Control4 reports it; nothing reported is "—"; readings without controls are sensors

**Context:** GitHub issue #75, from a home in the United States. A minisplit at 74 °F set to 69 °F showed "now 23°, target 21°": the API has always been °C and the app showed it as it came, although the driver knew each thermostat's scale. A Nest showed a target of −18°: Thermostat V2 read the single setpoint (1149), which a thermostat with separate heat and cool setpoints never reports, so 0 °F became −18 °C. Two temperature and humidity displays on the thermostat proxy ("Bathroom", 73° and 30 % in Control4, nothing to set) showed "Undefined · now 0° · Undefined", a −18° target and mode buttons; a "Weather Driver" showed as a thermostat. On the owner's home a CoolMaster floor zone shows "Off · now 0°": it reports no room temperature, and its 0 was shown.

**Decision (1.10.2):**
- **No temperature setting anywhere.** Each thermostat is shown in its own scale, as Control4 reports it: its scale variable (1100), else Composer's `TemperatureScale` as Director reports it, else the scale its room temperature is reported in. What no one thermostat says (the weather card, weather schedules' thresholds and editor, "only if", refrigerators, Composer's printout) is in the project's scale: Composer's `TemperatureScale`, else the one most thermostats work in, else °C; a °C project without thermostats stays °C.
- **The API, additive.** A thermostat says `scale` (`C` or `F`) and `sensor`, and `humidity` when one is reported. A °F one also has its temperatures in °F, as reported: `current_temperature_f`, `target_temperature_f`, `heat_setpoint_f`, `cool_setpoint_f`, `setpoint_deadband_f`, and its range in whole °F inside the °C one (16–32 °C is 61–89 °F); a °C one has no `*_f` fields, so the owner's answers grow by `scale` and `sensor` only. PATCH also takes `target_temperature_f`, `heat_setpoint_f` and `cool_setpoint_f` (not with the °C field of the same value), converted unrounded, so a whole °F reaches a °F thermostat exactly. `GET /v1/system` says `temperature_scale`. The °C fields stay as they were for scripts and older apps.
- **A °F thermostat gets whole °F, in its scale's key.** Thermostat V2's single setpoint in a °F project is sent as `SET_SETPOINT_SINGLE {FAHRENHEIT = 69}` (until 1.10.1 `CELSIUS` to 0.1 in every project), as the heat setpoint (#19) and the Control4 thermostat proxy already were, and as other Control4 clients send it (the proxy hands its driver the value in every scale). °C projects send `CELSIUS` as before.
- **The app** keeps each thermostat in its own scale (`app/js/temperature.js`, where the lists come in): every card, tile, step and command works in the degrees it shows. In °F: whole degrees, ±1 °F a tap, the heat-cool gap in whole °F, and the PATCH names the `*_f` fields, so 69 °F stays 69 °F; in °C nothing changes (0.5 °C). Commands in a °F home mean °F ("set the living room to 72", "2 degrees warmer" is 2 °F); their answers say the °F. Temperature sensors are never offered to commands or scene steps.
- **Scenes keep °C, to 0.1.** The editor works in the project's scale and keeps a whole °F as its °C to 0.1 (69 °F is 20.6 °C, 70 °F is 21.1), which comes back as that °F exactly wherever it is rounded (0.1 °C is 0.18 °F): the driver sends 69 °F. Nothing is stored differently, so a 1.10.1 driver or app reads every scene, and a step made in 1.10.1 (0.5 °C) runs to the °F it always did.
- **Weather thresholds stay °C** (no migration). In °F the app shows the weather in whole °F, and keeps a whole-°F threshold as the °C of half a degree below it, rounded up to 0.1: "81° or hotter" is kept as 27.0 °C (80.6 °F). As the forecast is °C to 0.1, the threshold itself then counts exactly at the °F the user picked, as ADR-074 has it, measured as the app shows it: the rule runs when the weather card says 81° (27.0 °C, 80.6 °F) and not when it says 80° (26.9 °C, 80.4 °F); tested for every whole °F the editor offers (59–113) against every forecast. A threshold is kept as stored while its number is not changed (28 °C shows as 82° and stays 28), and within the controller's 15–45 °C. "Runs again only after it has cooled to X° or less" names the highest whole °F that surely is.
- **Not reported is "—", never 0° or −18°.** A proxy variable reads 0 until its driver reports a value, so a temperature read as exactly 0 in the variable read is "not reported": a real 0 °C reads 32 in the °F variable and a real 0 °F reads −17.8 in the °C one. A room temperature of exactly 0.0 °C, or −17.8 °C next to a °F variable at 0, is not reported either (no room is at exactly 0.0 °C); a setpoint outside 0–50 °C is not one any thermostat works to (−17.8 °C is 0 °F converted). The API says null; the app says "—" in rooms, Climate, Home and favorites ("Off · now —"), and a command that steps a setpoint not known says so; a missing value is never sent, never copied into a scene, and the first tap on − or + starts from the room temperature (or 72 °F / 22 °C). A real 0 °C target (1149 = 32) still shows. An app talking to an older driver treats that driver's 0 °C room temperature and a setpoint under −17 °C as not reported too.
- **A Nest's setpoints.** A Thermostat V2 zone whose single setpoint is not reported (0, or missing) also reads and watches its heat and cool setpoints (1132–1135), the deadband (1146/1147), the single setpoint in °C (1150) and its fan speed list (1121). When it is not heat-only and reports a heat or a cool setpoint, it works to them as the Control4 thermostat proxy does, with the same rules (moved to `thermostat_setpoints.lua`, shared): the heat setpoint in Heat, the cool one in Cool, both in Auto (`setpoints: dual`), `SET_SETPOINT_HEAT`/`COOL` in its scale, the deadband kept, the fan speeds it lists in its own spelling (a Nest's Auto and On, not the Low, Medium and High of a CoolMaster zone). Each setpoint stands alone: one not reported is null (in Cool without a cool setpoint the target is "—" and can't be set). Not confirmed on a real Nest: the official driver is not public; Snap One's proxy documents `HEAT_SETPOINT_CHANGED`/`COOL_SETPOINT_CHANGED` and `has_single_setpoint` false for such a thermostat (no `GET_STATE` state document for the proxy was found), and what 1.10.1 showed (0 °F) fits. A zone that reports neither keeps its single setpoint.
- **Sensors.** A Thermostat V2 proxy that lists no Heat, Cool or Auto mode and reports no setpoint is a temperature sensor: its reading (the room temperature from whichever of °F and °C it reports, even with no scale variable; its humidity from the variable named HUMIDITY, else 1138), no mode, setpoint or fan speed. PATCH answers 409; a scene step naming it skips it (`NOT_SUPPORTED`), and a step for a room's climate leaves it out; the app shows one compact row ("Bathroom · 73° · 30%") in Climate and the room, and its reading on Home and in favorites. It is decided again on every change, so a zone whose values arrive late is a thermostat again.
- **Weather drivers** on the thermostat proxy are left out of Climate: one whose name has the word "Weather" or whose driver's file has "weather" (it is listed among the devices DirectorLink does not control). It is the outdoor weather, which the weather card shows; as a sensor it would read as a room ("Weather Driver · 84°").
- **The owner's zones.** Zones in use on their single setpoint in °C start with exactly the reads, listeners and log line of 1.0.0, as tested since 1.1.0; only a zone whose single setpoint or room temperature is not reported reads more. The demo project's thermostats are all °C; `dev_server.py --fahrenheit` is a US home with the thermostats of #75.

**Why:** A US home thinks in whole °F, and a value converted to °C, rounded, and converted back is a different setpoint (73 °F could not be reached in 0.5 °C steps). Following Control4 needs no setting and can't disagree with the home's own touch screens. °C kept to 0.1 for scenes and schedules changes no stored data, so nothing needs a migration and a 1.10.1 driver or app keeps working with them. A threshold counted at the °F shown keeps ADR-074's promise in the terms the user sees: the card says 81° and the "81° or hotter" rule runs.

**Consequence:** A 1.10.1 app with a 1.10.2 driver shows °C as before, "—" (or nothing) where nothing is reported, sensors with no buttons, and no weather driver. A 1.10.2 app with a 1.10.1 driver shows °C as before and "—" for 0 °C and −18°. A scene made in °F runs on a 1.10.1 driver to the same °F on a proxy thermostat, and with `CELSIUS` to 0.1 on a single setpoint, as 1.10.1 always sent. Changed: an AC zone whose single setpoint reads 0 and that reports a heat or cool setpoint follows them (it showed −18°); a °F single setpoint is sent in °F. Wind stays km/h. Nothing new reaches the cloud (the new fields travel sealed, as every answer); no D1 migration, nothing to deploy but the driver and the app.

## ADR-075 — Nothing reaches production without the owner's approval, and anyone can check that what is served is the source

**Context:** A question on Reddit: how could someone on Cloudflare's side control many homes? Not through the relay, which cannot read sealed requests (ADR-029), but `docs/ACCOUNTS.md` ("What the lock does not protect") names the real path: the app's code. The page that seals the requests holds the device's keys, so whoever can deploy app.directorlink.io (or the console, or the website) could ship code that uses them in every browser that opens it. Until now `deploy.yml` read the repository secrets `CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN` and `CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID` on every push to `main` and on pull requests from branches of this repository (for preview versions), so a push to any branch could reach the token; `release.yml` published the driver on a push to `main` with no human step; and nothing told anyone whether what the sites serve is the public source.

**Decision:**
- **One environment, approved by the owner.** The job that deploys the sites (`deploy.yml`) and the job that publishes a driver release (`release.yml`) run in GitHub's `production` environment: the owner is its required reviewer, it deploys only from `main`, and only it holds the Cloudflare token and account id (environment secrets; the repository keeps none). Each run waits until the owner approves it (Actions → the run → Review deployments; `docs/BUILD.md`, "After a merge"). Both jobs also run only `if: github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'`.
- **No preview versions for pull requests**: they needed the token. A `check` job runs `wrangler deploy --dry-run` on each of the four folders instead, with no secrets (a dry run needs no token), for pull requests and before each deploy. The `previews` blocks leave the `wrangler.jsonc` files.
- **A release is tested before it is approved.** `release.yml`'s `build` job (read-only) runs the tests and checks and builds; the `publish` job (approved, `contents: write`) builds again from the same commit and publishes only if both files are byte for byte the tested ones (the build is reproducible, so no files need to pass between the jobs).
- **build.json.** Before each static site is deployed, the deploy writes into it `build.json`, `{"commit": "<full commit>", "built_at": "<ISO time, UTC>"}`, which is never committed and which each `.assetsignore` publishes. The short link (`github-link/`, a Worker script, not files) has none; it is deployed with the others, from the same commit.
- **A check anyone can run**: `node scripts/verify_live.mjs` (Node 22, no dependencies, no secrets). For each of the three sites, the commit in its `build.json` must be on `main`, and every file its folder publishes at that commit, as wrangler uploads it (its `.assetsignore` read as `.gitignore` rules; `_headers` and `_redirects` are Cloudflare's settings, not files), must be served with the same SHA-256, following redirects within the site (`/privacy.html` is served at `/privacy`); every header `_headers` sets must be on each, and every fixed redirect in `_redirects` must answer as written there (the drivers' download links). `github.directorlink.io` must lead where `github-link/worker.js` says. A site without a `build.json` is compared with main's latest commits that changed its folder (50 in a clone, 5 from GitHub's API). In a clone it reads the clone, fetching `origin`'s `main` when a site names a commit the clone lacks; anywhere else, GitHub's API and raw files. Exit code 0 only when everything is the same.
- **Every hour, and after each deploy**, `watch-live.yml` runs it from a full clone, with `contents: read` and `issues: write` only. A difference fails the run and opens one issue, "The live site differs from the source" (or updates the open one, commenting when the differences change); once all is the same again it comments there and closes it. Not alarms: a difference within 10 minutes of `built_at` (checked again once the deploy has settled), a network error (tried three times, then a warning), a site that turns the check away (401, 403). Alarms: a file, header or redirect that differs, or a `build.json` commit that is not on `main`.
- `scripts/check_sites.py` keeps it so: only `production` jobs deploy, publish a release, read a secret or write to the repository, and only from `main`; no preview uploads and no `pull_request_target`; each site gets a `build.json` its `.assetsignore` publishes and nobody commits; the watch runs on a schedule with exactly its two permissions.

**Why:** The approval is the step that was missing: code merged to `main` still needs the owner to send it to the browsers of every home, and the token is out of reach of every branch, pull request and other workflow, which could read it before. A public, byte-for-byte check turns "the code is open source" into something anyone can verify, and the hourly watch makes a change on Cloudflare's side (a deploy from outside GitHub, a file changed, a download link pointed elsewhere) visible within the hour.

**Consequence:** After each merge the owner approves the deploy and, for a release, its publication; until then the sites stay as they were (a run waits up to 30 days). The environment, its reviewer and its secrets must exist before this workflow first runs on `main`, and the repository secrets must go: GitHub would otherwise make an unprotected `production` environment on the first run, and repository secrets reach every workflow. What the cloud learns: nothing new; each site serves the commit and time it was built from, both public anyway. What remains (`docs/ACCOUNTS.md`): the owner's GitHub account, the Cloudflare account, the domain's DNS and registrar; a site could serve the check the source and a browser something else; a file beside the source's that nothing loads is not seen. A native app would remove this trust. Nothing changes in the driver, the app's behaviour or the Worker; no D1 migration.

**Changed (2026-10-09): a release does not run the tests again.** `release.yml`'s `build` job ran the whole driver suite in one job, the time-zone runs and the checks, about 20 minutes before the owner could approve, while Validate DirectorLink had already run the same tests on the same commit, in parallel, in 4 to 6. Now:
- `release.yml` starts when Validate completes on `main` (`workflow_run`, `types: [completed]`, `branches: [main]`) or by hand on `main` (`workflow_dispatch`); no longer on a push.
- Its first job, `gate` (`contents: read`, `actions: read`), runs only after a run of Validate that succeeded on a push to `main` of this repository: `conclusion == 'success'`, `event == 'push'`, `head_branch == 'main'`, `head_repository` this repository. A `workflow_run` runs in this repository's context with its token whatever started Validate, so these keep out a pull request's run (a fork's branch may be called `main` too), a fork's, a failed or cancelled one and another branch's. Every job works on that run's commit (`head_sha`, for the checkout, the version and `--target`): after a `workflow_run`, `github.sha` is `main`'s latest commit. Run by hand, it is `main`'s latest commit.
- After Validate, only a commit whose `VERSION` differs from its parent's is a release (`main` takes one squashed commit per pull request); otherwise the run says "nothing to release" and builds nothing. Run by hand, always. Before anything is built, `gate` asks GitHub's API whether the latest run of `validate.yml` on the commit, pushed to `main` of this repository, finished and succeeded, and whether the commit is on `main` (by hand nothing else says so; after Validate it is GitHub's word as well as the event's), and refuses a release that exists, as before.
- `build` (read-only) checks the API description against the driver, builds, checks the package and records the checksums. The tests, the time-zone runs and `check_contract.py` ran in Validate on that commit, and the build is reproducible, so this is the package Validate built and checked. `publish` is as before: `production`, the owner's approval, `contents: write`, built again and published only if byte for byte the files `build` checked.
- `scripts/check_sites.py` keeps it so: the two triggers, naming Validate by its name in `validate.yml`; the first job's condition word for word; the API check; every checkout on `env.SHA`, no `github.sha`, `--target "$SHA"`; no `always()`, `cancelled()` or `failure()` that would run a job after a failed one; only the publishing job may write, exactly `contents: write`, and it compares the checksums with `build`'s.

From the merge to "Waiting" is now about 5 to 7 minutes (Validate's 4 to 6, then about a minute and a half), was about 20. A release now waits for Validate: if Validate fails on the commit that changed `VERSION`, nothing is built or asked until it passes (re-run, the release then starts by itself); after a fix merged later, the owner runs the workflow by hand on `main` (`docs/BUILD.md`, "Release policy"). Every push to `main` shows a short release run. Nothing changes in the driver, the app, the sites or the Worker.

## ADR-074 — A weather threshold itself counts, in rules and "only if" alike, and the words say so

**Context:** A heat rule "hotter than 23°" ran at exactly 23.0° (`>=`, since 0.14.0), but "only if hotter than 23°" needed more than 23° (`>`), "only if the wind is below 20 km/h" less than 20 (`<`), and a rule was ready again only under 2° (10 km/h) below its threshold. The app, Composer and the docs said "hotter than", "stronger than" and "below" for all of them. Since 1.10.0 the weather is the forecast's hours interpolated and rounded to 0.1 (ADR-071), so exactly 23.0 is common: the owner's Shabbat AC rule at 23° runs at 23.0, where a condition of 23° would not. Found in the 1.10.0 review.

**Decision (1.10.1):** One rule everywhere, the owner's: the threshold itself counts.
- Weather rules as before: heat `above` °C or hotter, wind `above` km/h or more.
- "Only if": `hotter_than` is that temperature or warmer (`>=`, was `>`), `wind_below` that speed or less (`<=`, was `<`). `rain_expected` stays a 50% chance or more.
- Ready again: once it has cooled to 2° below the threshold or less, or the wind dropped to 10 km/h below it or less (`<=`, was `<`). The dry hour stays an hour or more; rain stays any precipitation (more than 0, not a threshold anyone sets).
- The words say so wherever a threshold is shown or chosen: the app in its four languages ("When it's 23° or hotter outside", "Only if it's 23° or warmer and the wind is 20 km/h or less", "or hotter", "or more", "or warmer", "or less" under the editor's numbers, "Runs again only after it has cooled to 21° or less"; in Hebrew "ומעלה" and "ומטה"), and so History, which says a schedule as the list does; Composer's printout (`heat 23C or more outside`, `wind 40 km/h or more`, `only if 28C or hotter and wind 20 km/h or less`); docs/SCHEDULES.md; the API's descriptions. The API's names stay (`above`, `hotter_than`, `wind_below`).

**Why:** One rule is what people expect and remember, and "or more" is what weather rules have always done; making everything strict instead would stop the owner's rule at exactly 23.0, where it has always run.

**Consequence:** Existing schedules keep their numbers; nothing is stored differently, and going back to 1.10.0 keeps every schedule. What runs differently, only at exactly the threshold: a time, sun or Shabbat schedule with `hotter_than` 23 now runs at 23.0° (was skipped), one with `wind_below` 20 at 20.0 km/h; a weather rule is ready again at exactly 2° or 10 km/h below (was just under). A 1.10.0 app with a 1.10.1 driver still says "hotter than"; a 1.10.1 app with a 1.10.0 driver says "or warmer" for a condition that driver checks as "more than": they differ at the threshold only. Nothing new reaches the cloud; no D1 migration, nothing to deploy.

## ADR-073 — One 18 s budget for every relayed request; alerts sent again after a lost connection, pushed once

**Context:** Two leftovers of the 1.10.0 reviews (ADR-072). First, a request that found the driver reconnecting waited up to 8 s (`RECONNECT_WAIT_MS`) for its hello and then had the full 15 s (`REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS`) for its answer: up to 23 s, while the app gives up after 20 s (`app/js/remote.js`) and says DirectorLink's servers could not be reached, though the controller may still carry the request out. Second, ADR-072 covered only what the relay sends: an alert the controller sends on its own (`notify`: a doorbell rang, a camera saw someone, a door or gate was opened, the refrigerator's door, a schedule failed, and an ask-to-open link's question, ADR-058) written into a connection that had died without anyone noticing was lost, and so was one made in the second the driver was reconnecting. An ask-to-open link's run sent again by the relay even answered "asked" for a question that never left (1.10.0 review, finding 8). The owner's connection blinks every 20 to 60 minutes (ADR-072's Context), so a ring in those seconds was lost too.

**Decision (1.10.1):**
- **One budget per request** (`cloud/src/home-relay.js`, `REQUEST_BUDGET_MS` 18 s from the moment a request reaches the home's object): the wait for the driver (`liveDriver`), the first send, the sends again of ADR-072 and every wait for the answer each end at the budget at the latest; a request with nothing left is not sent. Every path that waits takes it: sealed requests, joins and claims (`message`), scene link runs (`link`) and the test endpoints (`forward`). The relay's `504 HOME_TIMEOUT` then says how long it waited ("The home did not answer within 18 s"), and reaches the app before its 20 s, so the app says the home is not connected right now ("Your home is not connected to DirectorLink right now…", `errors.remote.homeOffline`, as for every `HOME_TIMEOUT`), not that the servers could not be reached. `REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` (15 s) and `RECONNECT_WAIT_MS` (8 s) still bound their own parts, so a 1.9.0 or 1.10.0 driver sees the same waits, only never more than 18 s in all.
- **The driver keeps each alert until the relay answers it** (`driver/src/cloud/outbox.lua`, `Relay.alert`). Each `notify` gets `"id"`, 16 random hex digits, and is kept, its text made once, until the relay answers `{"type":"notify_result","id","ok"}`. After a reconnect, once the relay says it answers alerts, what was not answered goes again, oldest first, the same text with `"resent": n`; an alert made while the connection is down, within 2 minutes of losing a connection whose relay answered alerts (`Relay.KEEP_WINDOW_SECONDS`, a one-shot timer started at the loss, again at each such loss), is sealed and kept too, and goes then (so `Alerts` seals it although not connected: `Relay.mayAlert`); after those 2 minutes, as in 1.10.0, an alert while not connected is not made at all. Only an alert that went to a relay known to answer alerts goes again (each kept alert records whether the relay it last went to had said so, then or later on that connection): one that went while the relay had not said so yet, on a connection that then ended, is let go, because that relay may have been one before 1.10.1, which pushed it and ignored its id. An alert kept counts toward the hourly limits (`PER_HOUR`, `CAMERA_PER_HOUR`) as one sent, and is given back if it is let go before it ever went, so an outage uses up no limit; the alerts log says `alert kept for the next connection`, not `alert sent`. Bounds, by kind (`Alerts.KEEP_SECONDS`): a doorbell's ring and a door's question 60 s, because they are brief (the push service keeps them a minute), a visitor does not wait longer and the question lasts two minutes in all; a camera's alert, a door opened, the refrigerator's door and a schedule that failed 120 s, because they are worth knowing late, but the outbox is for reconnects of seconds and a short outage, and a home away longer is the offline alert's (ADR-047) and its history says what happened. Each alert's own one-shot timer counts it (real time, whatever the controller clock does, also while disconnected). At most 20 alerts and 128 KB together, the oldest let go first; memory only, so a driver that restarts sends nothing again.
- **The relay pushes an id once** (`cloud/src/alerts.js`). It records the id before it pushes, in the home object's storage (`notify_ids`: 10 minutes, at most 200, oldest first; read into memory once per object), answers `notify_result` at once, and pushes; an id it has already is answered and not pushed. Storage, not memory, because the object may be evicted while the driver reconnects (its sockets hibernate) or restarted by a deploy between the two sends; 10 minutes because the driver's 2 minutes are counted by timers that may run late on a busy controller, and 200 because a driver sends at most 60 alerts an hour. Check and record have nothing awaited between them, so the same id on two sockets at once is pushed once. Recorded before the push, so a failure in between loses an alert rather than doubling it. One that is not sealed parts is answered `ok: false, INVALID_REQUEST`. The ids are checked before the hourly limit, so an alert sent again never counts twice.
- **Both sides say so.** The driver's hello lists `alert_acks`; to such a driver a relay from 1.10.1 sends `{"type":"relay_features","id","features":["alert_acks"]}` at once, before the hello's storage write, so before anything else that hello lets it send (`accounts`, `alerts_gone`, requests sent again). The driver resends only on that word. It takes a relay heard without it as one that does not answer alerts: its `accounts` or `alerts_gone` before any `relay_features` (they follow the driver's `keys`, so come after a `relay_features`), or the second keep-alive tick after anything was heard on the connection (a `pong`), 5 to 10 s, not the first, so that a `relay_features` slowed on its way still counts. Then it lets go what it kept (never sent to a later relay, which could push it a second time), and sends its alerts once, without an id, as 1.10.0 did; while down, it keeps nothing. A connection that ends before the relay was heard on it decides nothing, so a second blink right after the first (ADR-072) loses no alert that was kept or had gone to a relay that said it answers alerts.
- **Ask-to-open (1.10.0 review, finding 8):** its question is such an alert, kept 60 s, so a run the relay sends again (answered "asked" from the driver's memory) now has its question delivered too.
- **Checked and left alone:** the driver's own questions have their own answers and retries, and nothing in them is plainly broken: `invitation` (no answer within 10 s: the invitation is revoked, `invitation_cancel` tells the relay, and the app gets `503 REMOTE_OFFLINE`), `owner` (`owner_cancel` kept for the next hello, ADR-064), `backup_chunk` (the backup stops, the nightly one is tried again every 15 minutes until 06:00).

**Why:** The budget is the whole request's because only the whole counts for the app; cutting each part separately would still add up past 20 s. 18 s leaves the Worker's own work and the way back 2 s. For alerts, an id and an answer are the least that makes "arrives once" possible over a connection that can die silently: the driver cannot know whether a frame arrived, so it must be able to send it again, and only the relay can tell an alert it has from one it has not. The driver's memory is short and bounded because a CORE-1 runs one Lua thread with little memory, and a late doorbell is worse than none. The relay's word after the hello, rather than the hello alone, because a relay rolled back to 1.10.0 between two connections would push an alert sent again a second time; the driver resends only on a word from the relay it is about to send to.

**Consequence:** A ring, a camera's or a door's alert in the seconds of a blink arrives once, a few seconds late, instead of being lost; one made while the driver reconnects arrives too. Still lost: an alert when the driver restarts, when the home is away longer than its minute or two (and any made more than 2 minutes after the connection was lost), beyond 20 waiting, one that went in the moment between a connection's hello and the relay's `relay_features` on a connection that then died (the driver cannot tell that relay's version, and a loss is better than a second push), and, as before, everything while the relay does not answer alerts. An outage of any length uses up no hourly limit, and costs at most 2 minutes of alerts sealed and kept. The relay never pushes an id twice: it pushes only after the id is written, and a write that fails pushes nothing and answers nothing (the driver sends it again at its next connection); and a 1.10.1 driver sends nothing again to a relay that did not say it answers alerts. What the cloud learns: a random id per alert, kept 10 minutes, and whether an alert came again (that the connection was lost while it was on its way: it saw each connection end already); never more about what it is (docs/ACCOUNTS.md). The controller keeps its own sealed alerts in memory for up to 2 minutes (at most 128 KB). The Worker must be deployed before the driver is updated: a 1.10.1 driver on the 1.10.0 Worker sends its alerts once, with an id the Worker ignores, as 1.10.0; a 1.9.0 or 1.10.0 driver on the 1.10.1 Worker lists no `alert_acks`, sends no id and gets no `relay_features` or `notify_result`, as before, and only sees the 18 s budget. No D1 migration, no new var or secret. A 1.10.0 or 1.9.0 app needs nothing new; it shows the relay's `HOME_TIMEOUT` as "home offline", as it always has.

**Review of 1.10.1 (rv-acks):** alerts were kept, sealed and counted toward the hourly limits for as long as the connection was down, so a long outage used up `CAMERA_PER_HOUR` and `PER_HOUR` and the first alerts after it were refused (`limit`): now kept only within the 2 minutes after the loss, and counted only once sent. An alert that went while the relay had not yet said whether it answers alerts was sent again to the next relay, which pushed it a second time when the first was one before 1.10.1 (a Worker rolled back and forward again): now only one that went to a relay that said so goes again. And the first keep-alive tick after a `pong` could take a slow 1.10.1 relay for an old one and let go what was kept: now the second. The tests' `RESEND_WITHIN_MS` (`.dev.vars` only, 10 s by default) lets `tests/cloud/budget.test.mjs` see the budget end a request sent again, which it never does with the production values.

## ADR-072 — A press is never lost when the connection blinks: the relay sends it again, the driver runs it once

**Context:** On the owner's home the relay connection died without a close every 20 to 60 minutes, all day and at night. Measured on 2026-10-06: the home's internet provider routes the home's traffic to Cloudflare through changing data centers (Tel Aviv, Geneva, Zurich, Marseille, Munich; a probe saw it flip between Tel Aviv and Geneva every few seconds). When the route moves, the open TCP connection reaches a data center that has no state for it and is reset, while the relay's side hears nothing. The driver learned it at its next ping (Director refuses the send: `connection lost`, `heard_s` about one interval, `ping_s` 0) and was back within 1 to 2 s, but a request the relay had sent into the dead connection failed with `502 HOME_DISCONNECTED` and was never sent again, because the controller might have carried it out (ADR-045). So a press in those seconds was lost: at about 21:01 the owner pressed Turn off all AC and nothing happened (the cut was at 21:01:05, `heard_s` 10, `ping_s` 0). Composer driver updates are not the cause, and nothing DirectorLink does can keep the route still.

**Decision (1.10.0):**
- **The driver runs each relayed request once, and can give its answer again** (`src/cloud/answers.lua`). For `e2e`, `join`, `claim` and `link` it remembers the relay's id of every request it got, for 120 s, at most 512 (ids only), and, once answered, the answer as it was sent, at most 64 answers and 512 KB together; an answer over 16 KB (a camera picture, a long list; a command answers in well under 2 KB) is not kept and the request is remembered as done. To make room, answers older than 30 s go first (the relay no longer asks for them), then the largest, so pictures and lists never push out a press's answer. The same id again, whether the relay marks it `resent` or not: a kept answer goes out again byte for byte and nothing runs; a request still running gets nothing now, and its answer goes, when it is done, on the connection there is then (the relay connection's `send` always writes to the current one); one done whose answer is not kept gets `{"type":…,"id":…,"ok":false,"code":"ANSWER_NOT_KEPT"}`. An id it does not know runs, unless the relay marked it `resent` while the driver had to forget, to stay within 512, a request that came within the last 30 s (the relay sends again only within 10 s): then it may have run, and it gets `ANSWER_NOT_KEPT` instead. Its seconds are the relay connection's keep-alive ticks (5 s each), not the clock, as the silence rule counts: no change of the controller clock, forward, back or back and forth, can make it forget early, and while the connection is down nothing ages. The check comes before a sealed request is opened: a repeat never reaches the replay check, which still refuses the same sealed request under any other id. Memory only.
- **The hello says so:** `features` gains `resend`, and `instance`, 32 random hex digits made at each start of the driver. The relay sends a request again only to a driver whose hello lists `resend` and names the instance the request went to: one that restarted (a Composer update, a reboot) remembers nothing, so it is not sent what went to the one before. Were it sent all the same, a sealed request is refused there (`STALE`: sealed before the driver started; or `REPLAYED` for a device clock ahead, whose ids are saved, as since 0.10.0), and a door never opens twice; a scene link's run has no such check, which the instance makes unneeded.
- **The relay keeps what was in flight** (`cloud/src/home-relay.js`). When the connection a request went on closes, fails, or is replaced by the driver's next one, a request sent to a driver that takes `resend` (as that connection's hello says when it ends, so also one sent in the moment between a new connection's upgrade and its hello) waits for the next hello, at most `RECONNECT_WAIT_MS` (8 s), instead of failing; at that hello, from the same instance, the exact same frame goes again with `"resent": 1` (or 2), the same id and the same sealed body, and waits 8 s (at most `REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS`) for its answer. At most twice, and only within 10 s of the request reaching the relay. Otherwise (no feature, another instance, no hello in time, a third end) it fails as before, `502 HOME_DISCONNECTED`; so does `ANSWER_NOT_KEPT`. Each hello that sends requests again logs `request_resent` (how many, the most sends again, how long after their connection was found gone; never a body).
- **What goes again:** `e2e` (every sealed request: a door's pulse, a scene, Turn off all, a setting), `join` (the same new key, sealed as it was: the invitation was used once), `claim` (the same `ok`: the token was used once) and `link` (a scene link's run, an ask-to-open question). Each is safe by the driver's memory, not by luck or by what it does. Not version 0's test `request` (refused anyway). `accounts` and `alerts_gone` have no answer and are sent again after the next `keys` as before. `invitation_result`, `backup_result` and `owner_result` answer the driver's own questions, which have their own waits and undo (the invitation revoked and cancelled, the nightly backup tried again later that night, `owner_cancel`).
- **A ping every 5 s** instead of 10 s (`Relay.KEEPALIVE_MS`, the hello's `ping_s` 5): a cut is found within 5 s. The relay's stale rule (2.5 intervals from the hello) becomes 12.5 s, the driver's silence rule (three pings) about 15 s. One timer on Director's Lua thread, a 10-byte frame each time; the pong is the relay runtime's auto-response, which Cloudflare does not bill.
- **A second blink right after the first** (the route flipping again): after a connection up a minute is lost, the driver connects again after 1 s, and so for the next two connections that open and are lost within a minute (`Relay.QUICK_IN_ROW`); then the backoff, as before for a connection that fails as soon as it opens. A connection that follows a lost one pings every second for its first 4 s (`Relay.WATCH_MS`, `WATCH_PINGS`): Director refuses a ping into a cut connection, so a second cut is found within a second, not at the first keep-alive 5 s later.
- **Times end to end:** the driver finds the cut within 5 s and is back after 1 s and a TLS handshake (measured: back within 1 to 2 s), so a resend goes within about 7 s of the request reaching the relay; a second cut right after is found within a second and the driver is back 1 s later again, so the second resend goes within 10 s while each reconnect's TLS handshake and upgrade take under a second, as measured. A resent request waits at most 8 s for that hello, is sent again within 10 s of reaching the relay and waits 8 s for its answer (`RESEND_TIMEOUT_MS`, under the 15 s of a request): answered within 18 s, under the 20 s the app waits for a request through the account (`app/js/remote.js`), which is unchanged.
- The scene link page's `nobody` text says Settings → Alerts (where alerts are since 1.10.0, ADR-067).

**Why:** Sending a request again is safe only if running it twice is impossible, and only the controller can know what it ran. Remembering ids and answers on the controller, for the seconds a reconnect takes, makes every kind of request safe the same way, doors and Turn off all included, without sorting requests into ones safe to repeat and ones not. The instance closes the one gap memory cannot: a driver that restarted, which the relay cannot tell from one that reconnected. Two resends cover a second blink right after the first (the route was seen to flip every few seconds), which is why the driver comes back at once after it too and watches a new connection's first seconds; more would not fit the app's 20 s. Sending again only within the reconnect wait keeps the driver's memory short. Pinging every 5 s halves the time a press waits and costs nothing on Cloudflare. A request the driver could not answer from memory fails exactly as before, so the app needs no change.

**Consequence:** A press in the seconds of a blink is carried out once, a few seconds late, instead of lost. The Worker must be deployed before the driver is updated; it is backward compatible: a 1.9.0 driver lists no `resend` and gets 1.9.0's behaviour, a 1.10.0 driver with a 1.9.0 Worker is never sent anything again and only pings more often (the old Worker counts its `ping_s` 5 as well). A 1.9.0 or 1.10.0 app needs nothing new. No D1 migration. The relay learns one thing more: a random id per start of the driver, so when the driver restarted rather than reconnected (it saw each connection already); it keeps a request's sealed frame in memory a few seconds longer, as while any request waits. The controller keeps sealed answers in memory for up to 2 minutes (up to 512 KB). Still lost: a request when the driver does not come back within 8 s (or, after a second blink, not within the 10 s), restarted meanwhile, or the connection blinks a third time, and a picture's or a long list's answer (the app asks again anyway); each fails with `502 HOME_DISCONNECTED` as before. What the driver sends the relay on its own into a dead connection (an `alert`, a `notify`; its own questions have their own waits) is still lost, as before: only the relay's requests go again.

## ADR-071 — The weather is a saved forecast, read every 6 hours and used for 5 days

**Context:** Weather schedules and "only if" read Open-Meteo's current conditions every 15 minutes (ADR-030), and a reading older than 45 minutes counted as none: without the internet, weather rules stopped and "only if" did what `if_no_weather` said. The owner's "hotter than 23°, 08:30–23:00, only on Shabbat and holidays" turns the main ACs on, and on Shabbat nobody can fix anything: an internet outage on Friday would leave the house hot until Saturday night. The owner first asked to keep the next 5 days' hourly forecast and use it when fetches fail, then decided to use the forecast always and ask Open-Meteo as little as needed. Open-Meteo's forecast API gives hourly values (`hourly=`; temperature and wind at the hour, precipitation as the sum of the preceding hour), daily values from the location's midnight (`timezone=auto`), and times in seconds from 1970 with `timeformat=unixtime`.

**Decision (1.10.0):**
- **One request, every 6 hours:** `hourly=temperature_2m,precipitation,weather_code,wind_speed_10m`, `daily=temperature_2m_max,temperature_2m_min,precipitation_probability_max`, `timezone=auto`, `timeformat=unixtime`, `forecast_days=6` (Open-Meteo's days start at the location's midnight, so 6 hold the 5 days after any hour), with the location rounded to two decimals as before; no `current=`. Every 6 hours while an enabled schedule needs the weather or an app showed it in the last hour (as before, for whether to read at all); 4 requests a day. A failed read is tried again every 30 minutes (was 5), logged once; one that holds no hour for now is a failure ("unexpected answer"). At once when no saved forecast holds now (none yet, run out, read for another location), so at a start only when the saved one is older than 6 hours.
- **The weather is always the saved forecast's hour for now** (`src/core/weather.lua`): the temperature and the wind between the two hours around now (rounded to 0.1), the precipitation of the hour now (Open-Meteo's value at the hour's end) and `raining` when it is more than 0 (not the weather code: the owner's rule), the weather code at the start of the hour now (Open-Meteo's is the moment's, as the temperature), the day's high, low and chance of rain from the day that holds now. Weather rules, their re-arming (2°, 10 km/h, an hour dry: the forecast's hours), "only if" (`rain_expected` too), `GET /v1/weather`, the app and Composer all read it; nothing reads the current conditions any more. Interpolated rather than the hour's value, so that a rule turns when the forecast crosses its threshold, not up to an hour later.
- **Kept for 5 days**, across restarts, in a key of its own, `directorlink_forecast` (1.9.0's `directorlink_weather` is left alone): version 2, the time it was read, the rounded location it is for, its first hour and each hour's four numbers in lists from the hour that holds the read to the hour after 5 days later (about 122 hours, 3 KB), and each day as `[start, max, min, chance]`; replaced by each read. Used while it holds now: from 5 days after it was read, or a location other than the project's, there is no weather (`unreachable` when the last read failed, as before), until a read succeeds.
- **Where it says so:** `GET /v1/weather` gains `source` (`forecast`, or null without weather), `forecast_for` (the moment `current` is for), `forecast_until`; `fetched_at` is when the forecast was read, `detail` why the last read failed also while the saved one is used, and `current.wind_gusts` is null. The app's weather card adds "Forecast for 14:20, updated today 08:00" (English, Hebrew, Spanish, Italian). Composer's Schedule Status ends with `weather forecast from today 08:00` (or `no weather forecast yet`, `(Open-Meteo unreachable)`, `(no location)`) when schedules use the weather; Last Automation says `heat rule, 31C forecast`. The scheduler's `schedule ran` log line has `forecast_from` when the weather decided the run. Run notes are unchanged (`heat`, `wind`, `rain`, `late`, `no_weather`): every weather decision is the forecast's, so a note would say nothing.

**Why:** The same weather with or without the internet is what a home that runs itself on Shabbat needs, and 4 requests a day instead of 96 ask Open-Meteo's free service as little as the schedules need. A forecast a few hours old is as good for "hotter than 23°" as a measurement 15 minutes old, which is itself a model's value for a grid cell, not a thermometer at home. Five days hold a Shabbat with a two-day holiday and the days before it. The rounded location in the saved forecast, because a forecast for the old place must not run the new one's schedules.

**Consequence:** A forecast can differ from what happens, usually by a degree or two, and the timing of rain more: a rain rule may close the blinds an hour early or late, or for a shower that does not come. What leaves the home is unchanged: the controller sends Open-Meteo only the location rounded to two decimals (and its address, as any request), now 4 times a day at most; nothing reaches DirectorLink's servers (docs/ACCOUNTS.md). The controller also writes the saved weather 4 times a day instead of 96. The site's privacy page says "every 15 minutes" and should say every 6 hours. **DirectorLink 1.9.0** after a downgrade finds its own last reading under its key, too old to use (it counts as none after 45 minutes), reads the weather at once and writes over that reading only; back on 1.10.0 the saved forecast is still there, so a return without the internet still has the weather. A 1.10.0 driver ignores 1.9.0's reading (no forecast) and reads one at once. A 1.9.0 app ignores `source` and shows the forecast's hour as the weather now; a 1.10.0 app with a 1.9.0 driver shows the weather as before, without the forecast line. No D1 migration; no Worker change. Supersedes ADR-030's every 15 minutes.

## ADR-070 — On, as it was: a scene turns each AC on in its own last mode; Keep for the temperature and fan

**Context:** The owner's Shabbat and holiday scene turns on the living area's ACs: one by name and every AC of two rooms, each to cool at 20° with the fan on medium. The family sets each AC its own way and wants each back as they left it: "without changing, so each AC keeps its previous setting, just turn off to on". A climate step could only name one mode for all, and the app's editor always sent a temperature with a mode (the driver had always taken a mode alone). A command, "turn on the AC in the living room", asked which mode when the AC was off.

**Decision (1.10.0):**
- **Each thermostat's last mode.** DirectorLink remembers the last mode each thermostat was in that was not off, whoever set it (Control4's apps, a keypad, Composer programming, DirectorLink): the adapter manager tells `src/core/last_modes.lua` when a thermostat's adapter starts (the driver starting, a project refresh, a driver update) and after every change of its variables. It is any of the thermostat's own modes (its mode list) but off, as the API names it, `dry` too when the AC lists it (then nothing replaces it: see below); a value outside the list (such as `Undefined` while a zone's driver starts) is never remembered, and the last mode stays the one before. It is kept in a small store of its own, `directorlink_last_modes` (`{"version": 1, "modes": {"30": "cool"}}`), written only when a thermostat's last mode changes, for up to 200 thermostats (past that, those no longer in the project make room). Not in backups: it is state, seen again (a restore on a new controller starts with none). `GET /v1/thermostats` shows it as `last_mode`, null until seen.
- **The scene step `{"mode": "on"}`**, alone (with a temperature, setpoints or a fan speed it is `400`, as `off` is).
